					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">De facto sovereign state in the Horn of Africa</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the self-declared country. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Somaliland_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Somaliland (disambiguation)">Somaliland (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Somalia" title="Somalia">Somalia</a>.</div>

independence&#160;<div class="ib-country-sovereignty">from <a href="/wiki/Somalia" title="Somalia">Somalia</a></div></th></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><link </div></th><td class="infobox-data">1 July 1960<sup id="cite_ref-Y0P0l_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Y0P0l-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" href="#cite_note-Y0P0l-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr><tr class="mergedtoprow"><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header"><a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Somaliland" title="Geography of Somaliland">Area </a></th></tr><tr class="mergedbottomrow"><th id="cite_ref-:3_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>&#32;(<a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population" title="List of countries and dependencies href="#cite_note-2vjSE-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr class="mergedbottomrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><div class="ib-country-fake-li">•&#160;Per capita</div></th><td class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Somaliland" title="Telephone numbers in Somaliland">Calling code</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/%2B252" class="mw-redirect" title="+252">+252</a> <small>(Somalia)</small></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Somaliland</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> officially the <b>Republic of Somaliland</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> is an unrecognised <i><a href="/wiki/List_of_states_with_limited_recognition" title="List of states with limited recognition">de facto</a></i> <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">sovereign state</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Horn_of_Africa" title="Horn of Africa">Horn of Africa</a>, still considered internationally to be part of <a href="/wiki/Somalia" title="Somalia">Somalia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Somaliland lies in the Horn of Africa, on the southern coast of the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Aden" title="Gulf of Aden">Gulf of Aden</a>. It is bordered by <a href="/wiki/Djibouti" title="Djibouti">Djibouti</a> to the northwest, <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia" title="Ethiopia">Ethiopia</a> to the south and west, and <a href="/wiki/Somalia" title="Somalia">Somalia</a> to the east.<sup id="cite_ref-XJmEz_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-XJmEz-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Encyclopædia_Britannica_2002_p.8352_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica_2002_p.8352-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Its claimed territory has an area of 176,120 square kilometres (68,000&#160;sq&#160;mi),<sup id="cite_ref-KitGhrsM12_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-KitGhrsM12-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> with approximately 5.7 million residents as of 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_6-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The capital and largest city is <a href="/wiki/Hargeisa" title="Hargeisa">Hargeisa</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Somaliland" title="Government of Somaliland">government of Somaliland</a> regards itself as the <a href="/wiki/Succession_of_states" title="Succession of states">successor state</a> to <a href="/wiki/British_Somaliland" title="British Somaliland">British Somaliland</a>, which, as the briefly independent <a href="/wiki/State_of_Somaliland" title="State of Somaliland">State of Somaliland</a>, united from 1960 to 1991 with the <a href="/wiki/Trust_Territory_of_Somaliland" title="Trust Territory of Somaliland">Trust Territory of Somaliland</a> (the former <a href="/wiki/Italian_Somaliland" title="Italian Somaliland">Italian Somaliland</a>) to form the <a href="/wiki/Somali_Republic" title="Somali Republic">Somali Republic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Encyclopædia_Britannica_2002_p.835_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica_2002_p.835-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The area of Somaliland was inhabited around 10,000 years ago during the <a href="/wiki/Neolithic_age" class="mw-redirect" title="Neolithic age">Neolithic age</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The ancient shepherds raised cows and other livestock and created vibrant rock art paintings.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Throughout the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arabs" title="Arabs">Arab</a> immigrants arrived in Somaliland, including the Muslim <a href="/wiki/Sheikh" title="Sheikh">sheikhs</a> <a href="/wiki/Ishaaq_bin_Ahmed" title="Ishaaq bin Ahmed">Ishaaq bin Ahmed</a>, who founded the <a href="/wiki/Isaaq" title="Isaaq">Isaaq</a> clan, and <a href="/wiki/Abdirahman_bin_Isma%27il_al-Jabarti" title="Abdirahman bin Isma&#39;il al-Jabarti">Abdirahman bin Isma'il al-Jabarti</a> who founded the <a href="/wiki/Darod" title="Darod">Darod</a> clan, who both travelled from <a href="/wiki/Arabia" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabia">Arabia</a> to Somaliland and married into the local <a href="/wiki/Dir_(clan)" title="Dir (clan)">Dir</a> clan,<sup id="cite_ref-:2_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> which have been described as legendary stories.<sup id="cite_ref-Lewis1994_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lewis1994-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Also during the Middle Ages, Somali empires dominated the regional trade, including the <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_Ifat" title="Sultanate of Ifat">Sultanate of Ifat</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Adal_Sultanate" title="Adal Sultanate">Adal Sultanate</a>.
</p><p>In the 18th century, the <a href="/wiki/Isaaq_Sultanate" title="Isaaq Sultanate">Isaaq Sultanate</a>, a Somali successor state to the Adal Sultanate, was established by Sultan <a href="/wiki/Guled_Abdi_(Sultan)" title="Guled Abdi (Sultan)">Guled Abdi</a> at <a href="/wiki/Toon,_Somaliland" title="Toon, Somaliland">Toon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Lewisapd_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lewisapd-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The sultanate spanned parts of the <a href="/wiki/Horn_of_Africa" title="Horn of Africa">Horn of Africa</a> and covered most of modern-day Somaliland.<sup id="cite_ref-auto3_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto3-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> It had a robust economy and trade was significant at its main port of <a href="/wiki/Berbera" title="Berbera">Berbera</a> and the smaller port town of <a href="/wiki/Bulhar" title="Bulhar">Bulhar</a>, as well as eastwards at the <a href="/wiki/Frankincense" title="Frankincense">frankincense</a>-exporting port towns of <a href="/wiki/Heis_(town)" title="Heis (town)">Heis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Karin,_Sahil" title="Karin, Sahil">Karin</a>, and <a href="/wiki/El-Darad" title="El-Darad">El-Darad</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the late 19th century, the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">United Kingdom</a> signed agreements with the <a href="/wiki/Habr_Awal" title="Habr Awal">Habr Awal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Garhajis" title="Garhajis">Garhajis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Habr_Je%27lo" title="Habr Je&#39;lo">Habr Je'lo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Warsangali" title="Warsangali">Warsangeli</a>, <a href="/wiki/Issa_(clan)" title="Issa (clan)">Issa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gadabuursi" title="Gadabuursi">Gadabuursi</a> clans establishing a protectorate.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Issa-Salwe_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Issa-Salwe-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Dervish_movement_(Somali)" title="Dervish movement (Somali)">Dervishes</a> led by <a href="/wiki/Muhammad_Abdullah_Hassan" class="mw-redirect" title="Muhammad Abdullah Hassan">Muhammad Abdullah Hassan</a> were against the protection agreements signed with Britain with the Somali sultans. After a span of 20 years, the Dervishes were finally defeated in one of the first aerial bombardments in <a href="/wiki/Africa" title="Africa">Africa</a> in 1920 <a href="/wiki/Somaliland_campaign_(1920)" title="Somaliland campaign (1920)">Somaliland Campaign</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The largest of the clans, the <a href="/wiki/Dhulbahante" title="Dhulbahante">Dhulbahante</a>, who did not sign a treaty of protection with the British (due to the fact that the <a href="/wiki/Italian_Empire" title="Italian Empire">Italians</a> considered part of the Dhulbahante as subjects of the Italian-protected Sultan of the Majeerteen clan)<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> were the foremost proponents of the movement.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 26 June 1960, the protectorate gained independence as the State of Somaliland, before five days later voluntarily uniting with the Trust Territory of Somaliland, following its separate independence, to form the Somali Republic. A lawful union occurred between the two territories through their elected representatives.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> On 27 June 1960, the Legislative Assembly of Somaliland unanimously enacted an Act of Union with Somalia which stated that the two entities would forever remain united.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1961, Somalia took control of state institutions, which was rejected in the former State of Somaliland and resulted in the Somaliland residents boycotting the vote on the <a href="/wiki/Somali_constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="Somali constitution">Somali constitution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards201484_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards201484-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In December 1961, the <a href="/wiki/1961_revolt_in_Somalia" title="1961 revolt in Somalia">revolt</a> in the north was started by soldiers of the former State of Somaliland who took control of large cities in the north.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESamatarSamatar2005116_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESamatarSamatar2005116-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> A group of officers took control of the <a href="/wiki/Radio_Hargeysa" title="Radio Hargeysa">radio station</a> in Hargeisa, declaring the end of the unity between Somalia and Somaliland.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichards201484–85_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards201484–85-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELewis1963170_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis1963170-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 1981 the <a href="/wiki/Somali_National_Movement" title="Somali National Movement">Somali National Movement</a> (SNM) was founded, which led to the <a href="/wiki/Somaliland_War_of_Independence" title="Somaliland War of Independence">Somaliland War of Independence</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:03_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:03-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> In 1988, at the height of the war,<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> the regime in Somalia under the dictator <a href="/wiki/Siad_Barre" title="Siad Barre">Siad Barre</a> began a crackdown against the Hargeisa-based SNM and other militant groups, which were among the events that led to the <a href="/wiki/Somali_Civil_War" title="Somali Civil War">Somali Civil War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Locsg_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Locsg-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The conflict left Somalia's economic and military infrastructure severely damaged. Following the collapse of Barre's regime in early 1991, local authorities, led by the SNM, <a href="/wiki/Somaliland_Declaration_of_Independence" title="Somaliland Declaration of Independence">unilaterally declared independence</a> from Somalia on 18 May of the same year and reinstated the borders of the former short-lived independent State of Somaliland.<sup id="cite_ref-NYT_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-const_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-const-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since 1991, the territory has been governed by democratically elected governments that seek international recognition as the government of the Republic of Somaliland.<sup id="cite_ref-profile_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-profile-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-nI4lO_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nI4lO-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-0RE1o_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-0RE1o-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> The central government maintains <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Somaliland" title="Foreign relations of Somaliland">informal ties</a> with some foreign governments, who have sent delegations to Hargeisa.<sup id="cite_ref-NYT_47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-jwTog_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jwTog-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Qg1ly_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Qg1ly-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Somaliland is currently recognized by the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_China" class="mw-redirect" title="Republic of China">Republic of China</a> (Taiwan)<sup id="cite_ref-taiwanmofa_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-taiwanmofa-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> and hosts <a href="/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_in_Somaliland" title="List of diplomatic missions in Somaliland">representative offices</a> from several other countries, most notably Ethiopia.<sup id="cite_ref-RdFuh_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RdFuh-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> However, Somaliland's self-proclaimed independence has not been officially recognised by any UN member state or international organisation.<sup id="cite_ref-NYT_47-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-DaV6b_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DaV6b-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-T3L29_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-T3L29-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> It is the largest unrecognized state in the world by de facto controlled land area. It is a member of the <a href="/wiki/Unrepresented_Nations_and_Peoples_Organization" title="Unrepresented Nations and Peoples Organization">Unrepresented Nations and Peoples Organization</a>, an advocacy group whose members consist of indigenous peoples, minorities and unrecognised or occupied territories.<sup id="cite_ref-It6qP_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-It6qP-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Annali_del_Museo_civico_di_storia_naturale_di_Genova_(1895)_(18411100885).jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Annali_del_Museo_civico_di_storia_naturale_di_Genova_%281895%29_%2818411100885%29.jpg/220px-Annali_del_Museo_civico_di_storia_naturale_di_Genova_%281895%29_%2818411100885%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="196" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Annali_del_Museo_civico_di_storia_naturale_di_Genova_%281895%29_%2818411100885%29.jpg/330px-Annali_del_Museo_civico_di_storia_naturale_di_Genova_%281895%29_%2818411100885%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Annali_del_Museo_civico_di_storia_naturale_di_Genova_%281895%29_%2818411100885%29.jpg/440px-Annali_del_Museo_civico_di_storia_naturale_di_Genova_%281895%29_%2818411100885%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3396" data-file-height="3022" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Annali_del_Museo_civico_di_storia_naturale_di_Genova_(1895)_(18411100885).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Map of Somaliland in the <a href="/wiki/19th_century" title="19th century">19th century</a></div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Somalis#Etymology" title="Somalis">Somalis §&#160;Etymology</a></div>
<p>The name <i>Somaliland</i> is derived from two words: "<a href="/wiki/Somalis" title="Somalis">Somali</a>" and "land". The area was named when Britain took control from the <a href="/wiki/Khedivate_of_Egypt" title="Khedivate of Egypt">Egyptian administration</a> in 1884, after signing successive treaties with the ruling Somali Sultans from the <a href="/wiki/Isaaq" title="Isaaq">Isaaq</a>, <a href="/wiki/Issa_(clan)" title="Issa (clan)">Issa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gadabursi" class="mw-redirect" title="Gadabursi">Gadabursi</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Warsangali" title="Warsangali">Warsangali</a> clans. The British established a <a href="/wiki/Protectorate" title="Protectorate">protectorate</a> in the region referred to as <a href="/wiki/British_Somaliland" title="British Somaliland">British Somaliland</a>. In 1960, when the protectorate became <a href="/wiki/Independence_Day_(State_of_Somaliland)" title="Independence Day (State of Somaliland)">independent</a> from Britain, it was called <a href="/wiki/State_of_Somaliland" title="State of Somaliland">State of Somaliland</a>. Four days later, on 1 July 1960, Somaliland united with <a href="/wiki/Italian_Somaliland" title="Italian Somaliland">Italian Somaliland</a>. The name "Republic of Somaliland" was taken upon the <a href="/wiki/Somaliland_Declaration_of_Independence" title="Somaliland Declaration of Independence">declaration of independence</a> following the <a href="/wiki/Somali_Civil_War" title="Somali Civil War">Somali Civil War</a> in 1991.<sup id="cite_ref-const_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-const-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the Grand conference in <a href="/wiki/Burao" title="Burao">Burao</a> held in 1991 many names for the country were suggested, including <i>Puntland</i>, in reference to Somaliland's location in the ancient <a href="/wiki/Land_of_Punt" title="Land of Punt">Land of Punt</a> and which is now the name of the <a href="/wiki/Puntland" title="Puntland">Puntland</a> state in neighbouring Somalia, and <i>Shankaroon</i>, meaning "better than five" in <a href="/wiki/Somali_language" title="Somali language">Somali</a>, in reference to the five regions of <a href="/wiki/Greater_Somalia" title="Greater Somalia">Greater Somalia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Somaliland" title="History of Somaliland">History of Somaliland</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Prehistory">Prehistory</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Caves_in_Somaliland" title="Caves in Somaliland">Caves in Somaliland</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See the <a href="/wiki/Laas_Geel" title="Laas Geel">Laas Geel</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dhambalin" title="Dhambalin">Dhambalin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dhaymoole" title="Dhaymoole">Dhaymoole</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dhaymoole_Rock_art_in_Somaliland.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Dhaymoole_Rock_art_in_Somaliland.jpg/220px-Dhaymoole_Rock_art_in_Somaliland.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Dhaymoole_Rock_art_in_Somaliland.jpg/330px-Dhaymoole_Rock_art_in_Somaliland.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Dhaymoole_Rock_art_in_Somaliland.jpg/440px-Dhaymoole_Rock_art_in_Somaliland.jpg 2x" data-file-width="634" data-file-height="357" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dhaymoole_Rock_art_in_Somaliland.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Wild_animals" class="mw-redirect" title="Wild animals">Wild animals</a> depicted in the caves of <a href="/wiki/Dhaymoole" title="Dhaymoole">Dhaymoole</a>, many of which have gone extinct in the region</div></div></div>
<p>Somaliland has been inhabited since at least the <a href="/wiki/Paleolithic" title="Paleolithic">Paleolithic</a>. During the <a href="/wiki/Stone_Age" title="Stone Age">Stone Age</a>, the Doian and Hargeisan cultures flourished here.<sup id="cite_ref-IqTdh_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IqTdh-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> The oldest evidence of <a href="/wiki/Burial" title="Burial">burial</a> customs in the <a href="/wiki/Horn_of_Africa" title="Horn of Africa">Horn of Africa</a> comes from <a href="/wiki/Cemetery" title="Cemetery">cemeteries</a> in Somaliland dating back to the <a href="/wiki/4th_millennium_BC" title="4th millennium BC">4th millennium</a> <a href="/wiki/BCE" class="mw-redirect" title="BCE">BCE</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DaznH_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DaznH-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> The stone implements from the Jalelo site in the north were also characterized in 1909 as important artefacts demonstrating the archaeological universality during the Paleolithic between the East and the West.<sup id="cite_ref-dKhgU_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dKhgU-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to linguists, the first <a href="/wiki/Afroasiatic_languages" title="Afroasiatic languages">Afroasiatic</a>-speaking populations arrived in the region during the ensuing <a href="/wiki/Neolithic" title="Neolithic">Neolithic</a> period from the family's proposed <a href="/wiki/Afroasiatic_Urheimat" class="mw-redirect" title="Afroasiatic Urheimat">urheimat</a> ("original homeland") in the <a href="/wiki/Nile_Valley" class="mw-redirect" title="Nile Valley">Nile Valley</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-0djHT_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-0djHT-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> or the <a href="/wiki/Near_East" title="Near East">Near East</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-g0KkI_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-g0KkI-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Laas_Geel" title="Laas Geel">Laas Geel</a> complex on the outskirts of Somaliland's capital <a href="/wiki/Hargeisa" title="Hargeisa">Hargeisa</a> dates back around 5,000 years, and has <a href="/wiki/Rock_art" title="Rock art">rock art</a> depicting both wild animals and decorated cows.<sup id="cite_ref-nFvRY_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nFvRY-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Other <a href="/wiki/Cave_painting" title="Cave painting">cave paintings</a> are found in the northern <a href="/wiki/Dhambalin" title="Dhambalin">Dhambalin</a> region, which feature one of the earliest known depictions of a hunter on horseback. The rock art is in the distinctive Ethiopian-Arabian style, dated to 1,000 to 3,000 BCE.<sup id="cite_ref-KmKxL_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-KmKxL-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-vDpdF_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vDpdF-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, between the towns of <a href="/wiki/Las_Khorey" title="Las Khorey">Las Khorey</a> and <a href="/wiki/El_Ayo" title="El Ayo">El Ayo</a> in eastern Somaliland lies <a href="/wiki/Karinhegane" title="Karinhegane">Karinhegane</a>, the site of numerous cave paintings of real and mythical animals. Each painting has an inscription below it, which collectively have been estimated to be around 2,500 years old.<sup id="cite_ref-i3tZl_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-i3tZl-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-dyZtk_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dyZtk-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Antiquity_and_classical_era">Antiquity and classical era</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Somali_Architecture" class="mw-redirect" title="Somali Architecture">Somali Architecture</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Sheekh_Isaaq.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Sheekh_Isaaq.jpg/220px-Sheekh_Isaaq.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Sheekh_Isaaq.jpg/330px-Sheekh_Isaaq.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Sheekh_Isaaq.jpg/440px-Sheekh_Isaaq.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1152" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Sheekh_Isaaq.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The tomb of <a href="/wiki/Ishaaq_bin_Ahmed" title="Ishaaq bin Ahmed">Sheikh Isaaq</a>, the founding father of the <a href="/wiki/Isaaq" title="Isaaq">Isaaq</a> clan, in <a href="/wiki/Maydh" title="Maydh">Maydh</a>, Sanaag</div></div></div>
<p>Ancient <a href="/wiki/Pyramid" title="Pyramid">pyramidical</a> structures, <a href="/wiki/Mausoleum" title="Mausoleum">mausoleums</a>, ruined cities and stone walls, such as the <a href="/wiki/Wargaade_Wall" title="Wargaade Wall">Wargaade Wall</a>, are evidence of an old civilization that once thrived in the Somali peninsula.<sup id="cite_ref-Nthos_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nthos-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8J8SA_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8J8SA-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> This civilization enjoyed a trading relationship with <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Egypt" title="Ancient Egypt">ancient Egypt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mycenaean_Greece" title="Mycenaean Greece">Mycenaean Greece</a> since the second millennium BCE, supporting the hypothesis that Somalia or adjacent regions were the location of the ancient <a href="/wiki/Land_of_Punt" title="Land of Punt">Land of Punt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Nthos_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nthos-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-TB9hR_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TB9hR-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> The Puntites traded <a href="/wiki/Myrrh" title="Myrrh">myrrh</a>, spices, gold, ebony, short-horned cattle, ivory and <a href="/wiki/Frankincense" title="Frankincense">frankincense</a> with the Egyptians, Phoenicians, <a href="/wiki/Babylonia" title="Babylonia">Babylonians</a>, Indians, Chinese and Romans through their commercial ports. An Egyptian expedition sent to Punt by the <a href="/wiki/Eighteenth_dynasty_of_Egypt" class="mw-redirect" title="Eighteenth dynasty of Egypt">18th dynasty</a> Queen <a href="/wiki/Hatshepsut" title="Hatshepsut">Hatshepsut</a> is recorded on the temple reliefs at <a href="/wiki/Deir_el-Bahari" title="Deir el-Bahari">Deir el-Bahari</a>, during the reign of the Puntite King Parahu and Queen Ati.<sup id="cite_ref-Nthos_71-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nthos-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> In 2015, isotopic analysis of ancient baboon mummies from Punt that had been brought to Egypt as gifts indicated that the specimens likely originated from an area encompassing eastern Somalia and the Eritrea-Ethiopia corridor.<sup id="cite_ref-mnKa4_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mnKa4-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Camel" title="Camel">camel</a> is believed to have been domesticated in the Horn region sometime between the 2nd and 3rd millennium BCE. From there, it spread to Egypt and the <a href="/wiki/Maghreb" title="Maghreb">Maghreb</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-uepm3_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-uepm3-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> During the classical period, the northern <a href="/wiki/Barbara_(region)" class="mw-redirect" title="Barbara (region)">Barbara</a> city-states of <a href="/wiki/Mosylon" title="Mosylon">Mosylon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Opone" title="Opone">Opone</a>, <a href="/wiki/Heis_(town)" title="Heis (town)">Mundus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bulhar" title="Bulhar">Isis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Malao" title="Malao">Malao</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zeila" title="Zeila">Avalites</a>, <a href="/wiki/Essina" title="Essina">Essina</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nikon_(Somalia)" title="Nikon (Somalia)">Nikon</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Sarapion" title="Sarapion">Sarapion</a> developed a lucrative trade network, connecting with merchants from <a href="/wiki/Ptolemaic_Kingdom" title="Ptolemaic Kingdom">Ptolemaic Egypt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greece" title="Ancient Greece">Ancient Greece</a>, <a href="/wiki/Phoenicia" title="Phoenicia">Phoenicia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Parthian_Empire" title="Parthian Empire">Parthian Persia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sabaeans" title="Sabaeans">Saba</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Nabataean_Kingdom" title="Nabataean Kingdom">Nabataean Kingdom</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a>. They used the ancient Somali maritime vessel known as the <i><a href="/wiki/Beden" title="Beden">beden</a></i> to transport their cargo.<sup id="cite_ref-DzFJ8_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DzFJ8-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the <a href="/wiki/Nabatea#Roman_annexation" class="mw-redirect" title="Nabatea">Roman conquest of the Nabataean Empire</a> and the Roman naval presence at <a href="/wiki/Aden" title="Aden">Aden</a> to curb piracy, Arab and Somali merchants agreed with the Romans to bar Indian ships from trading in the free port cities of the Arabian peninsula<sup id="cite_ref-srJWa_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-srJWa-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> to protect the interests of Somali and Arab merchants in the lucrative commerce between the Red and Mediterranean Seas.<sup id="cite_ref-EHW_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EHW-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> However, Indian merchants continued to trade in the port cities of the Somali peninsula, which was free from Roman interference.<sup id="cite_ref-rNe4n_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rNe4n-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For centuries, Indian merchants brought large quantities of cinnamon to Somalia and Arabia from <a href="/wiki/Ceylon" class="mw-redirect" title="Ceylon">Ceylon</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Maluku_Islands" title="Maluku Islands">Spice Islands</a>. The source of the cinnamon and other spices is said to have been the best-kept secret of Arab and Somali merchants in their trade with the Roman and Greek world; the Romans and Greeks believed the source to have been the Somali peninsula.<sup id="cite_ref-fqoYZ_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fqoYZ-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> The collusive agreement among Somali and Arab traders inflated the price of Indian and Chinese cinnamon in North Africa, the Near East, and Europe and made the cinnamon trade a very profitable revenue generator, especially for the Somali merchants through whose hands large quantities were shipped across sea and land routes.<sup id="cite_ref-EHW_78-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EHW-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2007, more rock art sites with Sabaean and Himyarite writings in and around <a href="/wiki/Hargeisa" title="Hargeisa">Hargeisa</a> were found, but some were bulldozed by developers.<sup id="cite_ref-qDY7X_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-qDY7X-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_of_Islam_and_the_Middle_Ages">Birth of Islam and the Middle Ages</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Somali_aristocratic_and_court_titles" title="Somali aristocratic and court titles">Somali aristocratic and court titles</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ifat_Sultanate" class="mw-redirect" title="Ifat Sultanate">Ifat Sultanate</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Adal_Sultanate" title="Adal Sultanate">Adal Sultanate</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:YagbeaSionBattlingAdaSultan.JPG" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/YagbeaSionBattlingAdaSultan.JPG/220px-YagbeaSionBattlingAdaSultan.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="141" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/YagbeaSionBattlingAdaSultan.JPG/330px-YagbeaSionBattlingAdaSultan.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/YagbeaSionBattlingAdaSultan.JPG/440px-YagbeaSionBattlingAdaSultan.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2379" data-file-height="1524" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:YagbeaSionBattlingAdaSultan.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A 15th-century French artist's rendering of a battle between troops of the <a href="/wiki/Adal_Sultanate" title="Adal Sultanate">Sultan of Adal</a> (right) and King <a href="/wiki/Yagbe%27u_Seyon_of_Ethiopia" class="mw-redirect" title="Yagbe&#39;u Seyon of Ethiopia">Yagbea-Sion</a> and his men (left). From <i>Le livre des Merveilles</i>.</div></div></div>
<p>Various Somali Muslim kingdoms were established around this period in the area.<sup id="cite_ref-Lewispohoa_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lewispohoa-82">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> In the 14th century, the <a href="/wiki/Zeila" title="Zeila">Zeila</a>-based <a href="/wiki/Adal_Sultanate" title="Adal Sultanate">Adal Sultanate</a> battled the forces of the Ethiopian emperor <a href="/wiki/Amda_Seyon_I" title="Amda Seyon I">Amda Seyon I</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-qGEXu_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-qGEXu-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a> later occupied <a href="/wiki/Berbera" title="Berbera">Berbera</a> and environs in the 1500s. <a href="/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_of_Egypt" class="mw-redirect" title="Muhammad Ali of Egypt">Muhammad Ali</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pasha" title="Pasha">Pasha</a> of <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>, subsequently established a foothold in the area between 1821 and 1841.<sup id="cite_ref-Clifford_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clifford-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Sanaag region is home to the ruined Islamic city of <a href="/wiki/Maduna" title="Maduna">Maduna</a> near <a href="/wiki/El_Afweyn" title="El Afweyn">El Afweyn</a>, which is considered the most substantial and most accessible ruin of its type in Somaliland.<sup id="cite_ref-:05_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:05-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:022_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:022-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> The main feature of the ruined city includes a large rectangular mosque, its 3 metre high walls still standing and which include a mihrab and possibly several smaller arched niches.<sup id="cite_ref-:022_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:022-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Swedish-Somali archaeologist <a href="/wiki/Sada_Mire" title="Sada Mire">Sada Mire</a> dates the ruined city to the 15th–17th centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-:13_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:13-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_modern_sultanates">Early modern sultanates</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Dervish_movement_(Somali)" title="Dervish movement (Somali)">Dervish movement (Somali)</a>, <a href="/wiki/Isaaq_Sultanate" title="Isaaq Sultanate">Isaaq Sultanate</a>, and <a href="/wiki/British_Somaliland" title="British Somaliland">British Somaliland</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:302px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Isaaq_Flag.svg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Isaaq_Flag.svg/300px-Isaaq_Flag.svg.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="190" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Isaaq_Flag.svg/450px-Isaaq_Flag.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Isaaq_Flag.svg/600px-Isaaq_Flag.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="253" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Isaaq_Flag.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A banner used by the Adal Sultanate and later the Isaaq on key religious shrines</div></div></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Isaaq_Sultanate">Isaaq Sultanate</span></h4>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Early_modern" class="mw-redirect" title="Early modern">early modern</a> period, successor states to the Adal Sultanate began to flourish in Somaliland. These included the <a href="/wiki/Isaaq_Sultanate" title="Isaaq Sultanate">Isaaq Sultanate</a> and <a href="/wiki/Habr_Yunis_Sultanate" title="Habr Yunis Sultanate">Habr Yunis Sultanate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8ot6e_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8ot6e-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Isaaq_Sultanate" title="Isaaq Sultanate">Isaaq Sultanate</a> was a <a href="/wiki/Somali_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Somali people">Somali</a> kingdom that ruled parts of the <a href="/wiki/Horn_of_Africa" title="Horn of Africa">Horn of Africa</a> during the 18th and 19th centuries. It spanned the territories of the <a href="/wiki/Isaaq" title="Isaaq">Isaaq</a> clan, descendants of the <a href="/wiki/Banu_Hashim" title="Banu Hashim">Banu Hashim</a> clan,<sup id="cite_ref-Lewisapd_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lewisapd-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> in modern-day Somaliland and <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia" title="Ethiopia">Ethiopia</a>. The sultanate was governed by the Rer Guled branch established by the first sultan, Sultan <a href="/wiki/Guled_Abdi_(Sultan)" title="Guled Abdi (Sultan)">Guled Abdi</a>, of the <a href="/wiki/Eidagale" title="Eidagale">Eidagale</a> clan. The sultanate is the pre-colonial predecessor to the modern Republic of Somaliland.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto3_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto3-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to oral tradition, prior to the Guled dynasty the <a href="/wiki/Isaaq" title="Isaaq">Isaaq</a> clan-family were ruled by a dynasty of the Tolje'lo branch starting from, descendants of Ahmed nicknamed Tol Je'lo, the eldest son of <a href="/wiki/Ishaaq_bin_Ahmed" title="Ishaaq bin Ahmed">Sheikh Ishaaq</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Harari_people" title="Harari people">Harari</a> wife. There were eight Tolje'lo rulers in total, starting with Boqor Harun (<a href="/wiki/Somali_language" title="Somali language">Somali</a>: <i lang="so">Boqor Haaruun</i>) who ruled the Isaaq Sultanate for centuries starting from the 13th century.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> The last Tolje'lo ruler <a href="/wiki/Garad" title="Garad">Garad</a> Dhuh Barar (<a href="/wiki/Somali_language" title="Somali language">Somali</a>: <i lang="so">Dhuux Baraar</i>) was overthrown by a coalition of Isaaq clans. The once strong Tolje'lo clan were scattered and took refuge amongst the <a href="/wiki/Habr_Awal" title="Habr Awal">Habr Awal</a> with whom they still mostly live.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Sultan of Isaaq often called for <i>shirs</i> or regular meetings where he would be informed and advised by leading elders or religious figures on what decisions to make. In the case of the <a href="/wiki/Dervish_movement_(Somali)" title="Dervish movement (Somali)">Dervish movement</a> Sultan <a href="/wiki/Deria_Hassan" title="Deria Hassan">Deria Hassan</a> had chosen not to join after receiving counsel from <a href="/wiki/Sheikh_Madar" title="Sheikh Madar">Sheikh Madar</a>. He addressed early tensions between the Saad Musa and Eidagale upon the former's settlement into the growing town of Hargeisa in the late 19th century.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> The Sultan would also be responsible for organizing grazing rights and in the late 19th century new agricultural spaces.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> The allocation of resources and sustainable use of them was also a matter that Sultans concerned themselves with and was crucial in an arid region. In the 1870s there was a famous meeting between <a href="/wiki/Sheikh_Madar" title="Sheikh Madar">Sheikh Madar</a> and Sultan Deria proclaimed that hunting and tree cutting in the vicinity of Hargeisa would be banned <sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> The holy relics from <a href="/wiki/Aw_Barkhadle" class="mw-redirect" title="Aw Barkhadle">Aw Barkhadle</a> would be brought and the Isaaqs would swear oaths upon it in presence of the Sultan whenever fierce internal combat broke out.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Aside from the leading Sultan of Isaaq there were numerous Akils, Garaads and subordinate Sultans alongside religious authorities that constituted the Sultanate before some would declare their own independence or simply break from his authority.
</p><p>The Isaaq Sultanate had 5 rulers prior to the creation of <a href="/wiki/British_Somaliland" title="British Somaliland">British Somaliland</a> in 1884. Historically, Sultans would be chosen by a committee of several important members of the various Isaaq subclans. Sultans were usually buried at <a href="/wiki/Toon,_Somaliland" title="Toon, Somaliland">Toon</a>, south of Hargeisa, which was a significant site and the capital of the Sultanate during <a href="/wiki/Farah_Guled" title="Farah Guled">Farah Guled</a>'s rule.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
